I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.

Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.

It’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.

And then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.

I’ve always been the kind of guy who could get by if I had to, like in sales there are bad quarters and you grind through it, but lately I start the day feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious during the day, outside of when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. During the day I’m usually just trying to keep moving—emails, calls, the usual fires. Sales is kind of built on that low-level pressure anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s normal stress and what’s me being… off, I guess.\n\nI notice it more at night, honestly. During the day I can distract myself with work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain things or situations during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… avoiding? I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s anything specific I’m steering clear of, like places or meetings or whatever.\n\nI mean, there are calls I don’t exactly look forward to—nobody likes calling a client who’s annoyed, you know? But that’s kind of the job. I’ll sometimes put something off and then end up doing three other little tasks first, like cleaning up my inbox or messing with a spreadsheet that probably didn’t need messing with… but I don’t know if that’s “avoiding” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images from your past that come into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like images that just pop in from the past. I mean, I replay things, but it’s usually stuff from work or something I said wrong, not like old memories exactly.\n\nI guess sometimes random things come up—like a dumb conversation from years ago or something embarrassing from college—but I figure everybody gets that. Nothing that feels like… I don’t know, like it’s taking over or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s like my brain makes a whole highlight reel of ways I’ve been selfish. Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. But yeah, I feel guilty about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are the guilty feelings you've been having usually about something specific that happened?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not one specific thing, no… it kind of starts with one thing and then spreads.\n\nLike I’ll think about a late night at work, and then somehow I’m thinking about tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made, and then it all piles up into this bigger feeling that I’m just… selfish, I guess. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. It’s more like everyday things that my brain turns into evidence against me, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I don’t know, sometimes I wonder if I’m just not thinking of it the right way. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.

And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.

I’ll sometimes put something off and then end up doing three other little tasks first, like cleaning up my inbox or messing with a spreadsheet that probably didn’t need messing with… but I don’t know if that’s “avoiding” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images from your past that come into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like images that just pop in from the past. I mean, I replay things, but it’s usually stuff from work or something I said wrong, not like old memories exactly.\n\nI guess sometimes random things come up—like a dumb conversation from years ago or something embarrassing from college—but I figure everybody gets that. Nothing that feels like… I don’t know, like it’s taking over or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s like my brain makes a whole highlight reel of ways I’ve been selfish. Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. But yeah, I feel guilty about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are the guilty feelings you've been having usually about something specific that happened?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not one specific thing, no… it kind of starts with one thing and then spreads.\n\nLike I’ll think about a late night at work, and then somehow I’m thinking about tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made, and then it all piles up into this bigger feeling that I’m just… selfish, I guess. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. It’s more like everyday things that my brain turns into evidence against me, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I don’t know, sometimes I wonder if I’m just not thinking of it the right way. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or take care of daily responsibilities because of these guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, my forecasts and client prep have slipped here and there, and then my team has to kind of fill in the gaps. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll be sitting with them and still stuck in my head about what I messed up, and then I miss the actual moment. So yes, I guess it is making daily stuff harder, even if I’m still technically doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think so. I mean, my wife especially can tell when I’m not really there. She’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I missed half of it because I’m thinking about work, or what I screwed up, or some conversation from three days ago.

I’m just not sleeping right.\n\nI’ll lie there at night replaying work stuff—conversations, things I should’ve said differently—and then somehow it turns into arguments at home too. And then I’m up at like 3 or 4 in the morning, just awake. I’ve always been the kind of guy who could get by if I had to, like in sales there are bad quarters and you grind through it, but lately I start the day feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious during the day, outside of when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. During the day I’m usually just trying to keep moving—emails, calls, the usual fires. Sales is kind of built on that low-level pressure anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s normal stress and what’s me being… off, I guess.\n\nI notice it more at night, honestly. During the day I can distract myself with work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain things or situations during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… avoiding? I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s anything specific I’m steering clear of, like places or meetings or whatever.\n\nI mean, there are calls I don’t exactly look forward to—nobody likes calling a client who’s annoyed, you know? But that’s kind of the job. I’ll sometimes put something off and then end up doing three other little tasks first, like cleaning up my inbox or messing with a spreadsheet that probably didn’t need messing with… but I don’t know if that’s “avoiding” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images from your past that come into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like images that just pop in from the past. I mean, I replay things, but it’s usually stuff from work or something I said wrong, not like old memories exactly.\n\nI guess sometimes random things come up—like a dumb conversation from years ago or something embarrassing from college—but I figure everybody gets that. Nothing that feels like… I don’t know, like it’s taking over or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s like my brain makes a whole highlight reel of ways I’ve been selfish. Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. But yeah, I feel guilty about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are the guilty feelings you've been having usually about something specific that happened?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not one specific thing, no… it kind of starts with one thing and then spreads.\n\nLike I’ll think about a late night at work, and then somehow I’m thinking about tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made, and then it all piles up into this bigger feeling that I’m just… selfish, I guess. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. It’s more like everyday things that my brain turns into evidence against me, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I don’t know, sometimes I wonder if I’m just not thinking of it the right way. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. Yeah… I’ve thought about that. I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.

It’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.

And it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.

Nothing that feels like… I don’t know, like it’s taking over or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s like my brain makes a whole highlight reel of ways I’ve been selfish. Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. But yeah, I feel guilty about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are the guilty feelings you've been having usually about something specific that happened?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not one specific thing, no… it kind of starts with one thing and then spreads.\n\nLike I’ll think about a late night at work, and then somehow I’m thinking about tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made, and then it all piles up into this bigger feeling that I’m just… selfish, I guess. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. It’s more like everyday things that my brain turns into evidence against me, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I don’t know, sometimes I wonder if I’m just not thinking of it the right way. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or take care of daily responsibilities because of these guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, my forecasts and client prep have slipped here and there, and then my team has to kind of fill in the gaps. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll be sitting with them and still stuck in my head about what I messed up, and then I miss the actual moment. So yes, I guess it is making daily stuff harder, even if I’m still technically doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife and kids interact with you since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my wife especially can tell when I’m not really there. She’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I missed half of it because I’m thinking about work, or what I screwed up, or some conversation from three days ago.\n\nAnd with the kids, it’s more like… they still come up to me and want to show me stuff, but I’m slower to engage, I guess. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. It reminds me of when my dad used to watch TV during dinner—different thing, I know—but I hated that as a kid, and now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m still showing up at home, same as work, but I’m not as present as I used to be. And at work, when I let forecasts or client prep slip, the team ends up covering for me, which kind of follows me home too.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular event or situation that you feel is the main source of your guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one event exactly. It’s more like… I keep adding things up in my head.\n\nLike every late night I stayed at work and told myself it was necessary, every tailgate or thing I went to when maybe I should’ve been home, every dumb purchase where I convinced myself it wasn’t a big deal. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. And then I can’t really shake that.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your relationships with your wife and kids might be suffering because of your preoccupation with guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve thought about that. I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.\n\nIt’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.\n\nIt’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / sales_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep replaying every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase, and I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a selfish guy who keeps letting them down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I think there is. It’s not like one exact sentence, but it’s this loop of, “You’re selfish, you keep letting them down.”

And then my brain starts pulling up evidence, you know? Like every late night I stayed at work, or those tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made and brushed off at the time. Even stuff that maybe wasn’t that big, it gets added to the pile.

It’s weird, because I’ll be doing something normal, like sitting at the kitchen table or driving past the stadium—sorry, that sounds random, but those tailgates are tied into it—and suddenly I’m back to thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who can’t get his priorities straight. Like they deserved better than me. That’s the part I can’t seem to shake.

Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. But yeah, I feel guilty about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are the guilty feelings you've been having usually about something specific that happened?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not one specific thing, no… it kind of starts with one thing and then spreads.\n\nLike I’ll think about a late night at work, and then somehow I’m thinking about tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made, and then it all piles up into this bigger feeling that I’m just… selfish, I guess. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I guess. I still go to work, I still come home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.

But it’s definitely affecting things. At work, if I get stuck in my head, my forecasts aren’t as tight as they should be, or I’m not as prepared for a client meeting as I normally would be. And then my team has to kind of cover the gaps, which just makes me feel worse.

And at home… I’m there, but not the way I used to be. My wife can tell when I’m checked out, and the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I barely heard them. I hate that. I keep thinking about how my dad used to come home and just sit in his chair with the TV on—sorry, that’s probably not the point—but I catch myself doing that same kind of thing, just not really present. So yeah, it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but it’s definitely getting in the way.

Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. It’s more like everyday things that my brain turns into evidence against me, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I don’t know, sometimes I wonder if I’m just not thinking of it the right way. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or take care of daily responsibilities because of these guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, my forecasts and client prep have slipped here and there, and then my team has to kind of fill in the gaps. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll be sitting with them and still stuck in my head about what I messed up, and then I miss the actual moment. So yes, I guess it is making daily stuff harder, even if I’m still technically doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife and kids interact with you since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my wife especially can tell when I’m not really there. She’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I missed half of it because I’m thinking about work, or what I screwed up, or some conversation from three days ago.\n\nAnd with the kids, it’s more like… they still come up to me and want to show me stuff, but I’m slower to engage, I guess. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. It reminds me of when my dad used to watch TV during dinner—different thing, I know—but I hated that as a kid, and now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m still showing up at home, same as work, but I’m not as present as I used to be. And at work, when I let forecasts or client prep slip, the team ends up covering for me, which kind of follows me home too.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular event or situation that you feel is the main source of your guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one event exactly. It’s more like… I keep adding things up in my head.\n\nLike every late night I stayed at work and told myself it was necessary, every tailgate or thing I went to when maybe I should’ve been home, every dumb purchase where I convinced myself it wasn’t a big deal. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or take care of daily responsibilities because of these guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, my forecasts and client prep have slipped here and there, and then my team has to kind of fill in the gaps. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll be sitting with them and still stuck in my head about what I messed up, and then I miss the actual moment. So yes, I guess it is making daily stuff harder, even if I’m still technically doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife and kids interact with you since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my wife especially can tell when I’m not really there. She’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I missed half of it because I’m thinking about work, or what I screwed up, or some conversation from three days ago.\n\nAnd with the kids, it’s more like… they still come up to me and want to show me stuff, but I’m slower to engage, I guess. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. It reminds me of when my dad used to watch TV during dinner—different thing, I know—but I hated that as a kid, and now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m still showing up at home, same as work, but I’m not as present as I used to be. And at work, when I let forecasts or client prep slip, the team ends up covering for me, which kind of follows me home too.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular event or situation that you feel is the main source of your guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one event exactly. It’s more like… I keep adding things up in my head.\n\nLike every late night I stayed at work and told myself it was necessary, every tailgate or thing I went to when maybe I should’ve been home, every dumb purchase where I convinced myself it wasn’t a big deal. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. And then I can’t really shake that.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your relationships with your wife and kids might be suffering because of your preoccupation with guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve thought about that. I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.\n\nIt’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think there is. Like every late night I stayed at work, or those tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made and brushed off at the time. Even stuff that maybe wasn’t that big, it gets added to the pile.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be doing something normal, like sitting at the kitchen table or driving past the stadium—sorry, that sounds random, but those tailgates are tied into it—and suddenly I’m back to thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who can’t get his priorities straight. Like they deserved better than me. That’s the part I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning as a result of your guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I guess. Mostly it comes back to work and family, honestly. I mean, those are the big ones.

But it can spill over into other stuff too—like if I’m at a tailgate, or even just thinking about the money I spent on something stupid, I start doing this inventory in my head. Every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase… and somehow it all points back to the same thing, like, “See, you’re selfish.”

And then I’m not just feeling bad about that one thing. I’m thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down. So even if it starts outside of work or family, it usually ends up there.

I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or take care of daily responsibilities because of these guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, my forecasts and client prep have slipped here and there, and then my team has to kind of fill in the gaps. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll be sitting with them and still stuck in my head about what I messed up, and then I miss the actual moment. So yes, I guess it is making daily stuff harder, even if I’m still technically doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife and kids interact with you since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my wife especially can tell when I’m not really there. She’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I missed half of it because I’m thinking about work, or what I screwed up, or some conversation from three days ago.\n\nAnd with the kids, it’s more like… they still come up to me and want to show me stuff, but I’m slower to engage, I guess. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. It reminds me of when my dad used to watch TV during dinner—different thing, I know—but I hated that as a kid, and now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m still showing up at home, same as work, but I’m not as present as I used to be. And at work, when I let forecasts or client prep slip, the team ends up covering for me, which kind of follows me home too.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular event or situation that you feel is the main source of your guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one event exactly. It’s more like… I keep adding things up in my head.\n\nLike every late night I stayed at work and told myself it was necessary, every tailgate or thing I went to when maybe I should’ve been home, every dumb purchase where I convinced myself it wasn’t a big deal. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. And then I can’t really shake that.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your relationships with your wife and kids might be suffering because of your preoccupation with guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve thought about that. I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.\n\nIt’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think there is. Like every late night I stayed at work, or those tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made and brushed off at the time. Even stuff that maybe wasn’t that big, it gets added to the pile.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be doing something normal, like sitting at the kitchen table or driving past the stadium—sorry, that sounds random, but those tailgates are tied into it—and suddenly I’m back to thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who can’t get his priorities straight. Like they deserved better than me. That’s the part I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning as a result of your guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I guess. I still go to work, I still come home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. At work, if I get stuck in my head, my forecasts aren’t as tight as they should be, or I’m not as prepared for a client meeting as I normally would be. That’s the part I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning as a result of your guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I guess. I still go to work, I still come home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. At work, if I get stuck in my head, my forecasts aren’t as tight as they should be, or I’m not as prepared for a client meeting as I normally would be. And then my team has to kind of cover the gaps, which just makes me feel worse.\n\nAnd at home… I’m there, but not the way I used to be. My wife can tell when I’m checked out, and the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I barely heard them. I hate that. I keep thinking about how my dad used to come home and just sit in his chair with the TV on—sorry, that’s probably not the point—but I catch myself doing that same kind of thing, just not really present. So yeah, it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about things outside of work and family as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it comes back to work and family, honestly. I mean, those are the big ones.\n\nBut it can spill over into other stuff too—like if I’m at a tailgate, or even just thinking about the money I spent on something stupid, I start doing this inventory in my head. Every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase… and somehow it all points back to the same thing, like, “See, you’re selfish.”\n\nAnd then I’m not just feeling bad about that one thing. I’m thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down. So even if it starts outside of work or family, it usually ends up there.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there specific mistakes or behaviors from your past that you keep coming back to when you feel guilty or ashamed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / sales_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep replaying every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase, and I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a selfish guy who keeps letting them down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… there are a few that I keep circling back to.

Late nights at work are a big one. Even if I told myself it was for the family, I’ll replay it like, “You chose work again.” And then there were tailgates I went to where, looking back, I feel like I should’ve been home or doing something useful. I know that probably sounds small, but in my head it becomes this whole case against me.

And dumb purchases too—stuff I brushed off at the time, like, “It’s fine, I work hard,” but later I’ll think about what that money could’ve been for. Then it all kind of piles up, and I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.

Sorry, I know I’m kind of looping on the same examples, but those are the ones my brain keeps dragging back out.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what finally pushed me to call the employee assistance program was my sleep. I’m just not sleeping right.\n\nI’ll lie there at night replaying work stuff—conversations, things I should’ve said differently—and then somehow it turns into arguments at home too. And then I’m up at like 3 or 4 in the morning, just awake. I’ve always been the kind of guy who could get by if I had to, like in sales there are bad quarters and you grind through it, but lately I start the day feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious during the day, outside of when you're lying awake at night?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. During the day I’m usually just trying to keep moving—emails, calls, the usual fires. Sales is kind of built on that low-level pressure anyway, so it’s hard to tell what’s normal stress and what’s me being… off, I guess.\n\nI notice it more at night, honestly. During the day I can distract myself with work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain things or situations during the day?\n\nPatient: Um… avoiding? I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s anything specific I’m steering clear of, like places or meetings or whatever.\n\nI mean, there are calls I don’t exactly look forward to—nobody likes calling a client who’s annoyed, you know? But that’s kind of the job. I’ll sometimes put something off and then end up doing three other little tasks first, like cleaning up my inbox or messing with a spreadsheet that probably didn’t need messing with… but I don’t know if that’s “avoiding” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images from your past that come into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like images that just pop in from the past. I mean, I replay things, but it’s usually stuff from work or something I said wrong, not like old memories exactly.\n\nI guess sometimes random things come up—like a dumb conversation from years ago or something embarrassing from college—but I figure everybody gets that. Nothing that feels like… I don’t know, like it’s taking over or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. More than I probably should.\n\nIt’s not even always one big thing, it’s like my brain makes a whole highlight reel of ways I’ve been selfish. Like late nights at work when I told myself I had to stay, or tailgates where I acted like that was somehow important, or stupid stuff I bought that we didn’t need. And then I’ll be thinking about, I don’t know, the credit card bill or the kids needing something for school, and suddenly it turns into this whole thing where I’m like… my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nI mean, some of that is just being a parent, right? You’re always messing something up. But yeah, I feel guilty about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Are the guilty feelings you've been having usually about something specific that happened?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not one specific thing, no… it kind of starts with one thing and then spreads.\n\nLike I’ll think about a late night at work, and then somehow I’m thinking about tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made, and then it all piles up into this bigger feeling that I’m just… selfish, I guess. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI know that’s not exactly a single incident, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or disturbing events in your life that you think might be contributing to these guilty feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, nothing I’d call traumatic, really.\n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, sure—work things, family arguments, money stuff—but I don’t have some big event in mind that explains the guilt. It’s more like everyday things that my brain turns into evidence against me, if that makes sense.\n\nAlthough, I don’t know, sometimes I wonder if I’m just not thinking of it the right way. Like people say “trauma” and I think car accident or something dramatic, and that’s not really my story. Mine is more… I’ll remember a normal Tuesday where I was late getting home, and then suddenly I’m thinking about ten other things I screwed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors over and over, like checking or cleaning?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll double-check the door sometimes, or make sure I sent an email, but that feels pretty normal? Like at work I’ll reread something because one typo in a proposal can turn into this whole annoying back-and-forth with a client, and then you’re stuck explaining it in a meeting that should’ve been ten minutes but becomes forty…\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed myself doing a lot of checking or cleaning over and over like that. At least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these things you feel guilty about, do you also feel ashamed of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… shame is probably the word for it.\n\nIt’s not just “I did a bad thing,” it turns into “I’m that kind of guy,” you know? I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. And then it just kind of… stuck.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to do your job or take care of daily responsibilities because of these guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still showing up. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. But it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work, my forecasts and client prep have slipped here and there, and then my team has to kind of fill in the gaps. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll be sitting with them and still stuck in my head about what I messed up, and then I miss the actual moment. So yes, I guess it is making daily stuff harder, even if I’m still technically doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how your wife and kids interact with you since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my wife especially can tell when I’m not really there. She’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I missed half of it because I’m thinking about work, or what I screwed up, or some conversation from three days ago.\n\nAnd with the kids, it’s more like… they still come up to me and want to show me stuff, but I’m slower to engage, I guess. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. It reminds me of when my dad used to watch TV during dinner—different thing, I know—but I hated that as a kid, and now I catch myself doing my own version of it.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m still showing up at home, same as work, but I’m not as present as I used to be. And at work, when I let forecasts or client prep slip, the team ends up covering for me, which kind of follows me home too.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular event or situation that you feel is the main source of your guilty feelings and shame?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s one event exactly. It’s more like… I keep adding things up in my head.\n\nLike every late night I stayed at work and told myself it was necessary, every tailgate or thing I went to when maybe I should’ve been home, every dumb purchase where I convinced myself it wasn’t a big deal. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. And then I can’t really shake that.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your relationships with your wife and kids might be suffering because of your preoccupation with guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve thought about that. I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.\n\nIt’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think there is. It’s not like one exact sentence, but it’s this loop of, “You’re selfish, you keep letting them down.”\n\nAnd then my brain starts pulling up evidence, you know? Like every late night I stayed at work, or those tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made and brushed off at the time. Even stuff that maybe wasn’t that big, it gets added to the pile.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be doing something normal, like sitting at the kitchen table or driving past the stadium—sorry, that sounds random, but those tailgates are tied into it—and suddenly I’m back to thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who can’t get his priorities straight. Like they deserved better than me. That’s the part I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning as a result of your guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I guess. I still go to work, I still come home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. At work, if I get stuck in my head, my forecasts aren’t as tight as they should be, or I’m not as prepared for a client meeting as I normally would be. And then my team has to kind of cover the gaps, which just makes me feel worse.\n\nAnd at home… I’m there, but not the way I used to be. My wife can tell when I’m checked out, and the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I barely heard them. I hate that. I keep thinking about how my dad used to come home and just sit in his chair with the TV on—sorry, that’s probably not the point—but I catch myself doing that same kind of thing, just not really present. So yeah, it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about things outside of work and family as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it comes back to work and family, honestly. I mean, those are the big ones.\n\nBut it can spill over into other stuff too—like if I’m at a tailgate, or even just thinking about the money I spent on something stupid, I start doing this inventory in my head. Every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase… and somehow it all points back to the same thing, like, “See, you’re selfish.”\n\nAnd then I’m not just feeling bad about that one thing. I know that probably sounds small, but in my head it becomes this whole case against me.\n\nAnd dumb purchases too—stuff I brushed off at the time, like, “It’s fine, I work hard,” but later I’ll think about what that money could’ve been for. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think there is. Even saying that sounds dramatic, because nobody’s sitting me down and saying that to me, but that’s where my head goes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself constantly thinking about a specific thought or idea that triggers your feelings of guilt and shame?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / sales_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep replaying every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase, and I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a selfish guy who keeps letting them down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… there is kind of one thought, I guess. It’s not like a single sentence every time, but it’s that same idea that I’m selfish, that I’ve been selfish.

Like I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, every dumb thing I bought when I probably should’ve been thinking about the family. And then my mind just… connects all of it, even stuff that doesn’t really belong together.

I’ll be driving or trying to do something totally normal, and suddenly it’s like, “Your wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.” That’s the one that really gets me. I know I keep saying it, but it just keeps coming back around.

I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, some stupid purchase I made—stuff that on its own maybe isn’t the end of the world—and somehow I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish person who keeps letting them down.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about, like, other dads who seem to have it all balanced, which I know is dumb because you don’t actually know what’s going on in someone else’s house… but yeah, I feel ashamed of myself when I get on that track.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself, with the guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over a year? Maybe about a year and two months, if I’m counting it right.\n\nIt started getting more noticeable when the arguments at home got a lot worse. Not that it was like one day everything changed, but around then I started feeling like every fight was proof I was failing somehow. I haven’t, like, stopped working or anything. Which makes me feel worse, obviously, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping things moving. And sales is one of those jobs where if you’re a little off, everybody notices—well, maybe not everybody, but it can snowball fast.\n\nAt home too… I’m there, but I’m not as present with my wife and kids as I used to be. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, buddy, that’s cool,” and then feel terrible because I barely looked. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. And then I can’t really shake that.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your relationships with your wife and kids might be suffering because of your preoccupation with guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve thought about that.
