My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think there is. It’s not like one exact sentence, but it’s this loop of, “You’re selfish, you keep letting them down.”\n\nAnd then my brain starts pulling up evidence, you know? Like every late night I stayed at work, or those tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made and brushed off at the time. So yeah, it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about things outside of work and family as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it comes back to work and family, honestly. I mean, those are the big ones.\n\nBut it can spill over into other stuff too—like if I’m at a tailgate, or even just thinking about the money I spent on something stupid, I start doing this inventory in my head. Every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase… and somehow it all points back to the same thing, like, “See, you’re selfish.”\n\nAnd then I’m not just feeling bad about that one thing. I’m thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down. So even if it starts outside of work or family, it usually ends up there.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific mistakes or behaviors from your past that you keep coming back to when you feel guilty or ashamed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are a few that I keep circling back to.\n\nLate nights at work are a big one. I know that probably sounds small, but in my head it becomes this whole case against me.\n\nAnd dumb purchases too—stuff I brushed off at the time, like, “It’s fine, I work hard,” but later I’ll think about what that money could’ve been for. It’s more like… I keep adding things up in my head.\n\nLike every late night I stayed at work and told myself it was necessary, every tailgate or thing I went to when maybe I should’ve been home, every dumb purchase where I convinced myself it wasn’t a big deal. And then I start replaying all of it, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nIt’s stupid because some of those things probably aren’t huge on their own, but in my head they turn into this picture where my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down. And then I can’t really shake that.\n\nDoctor: Have you considered that your relationships with your wife and kids might be suffering because of your preoccupation with guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve thought about that. I mean, not always in that exact wording, but yes.\n\nIt’s like I’m physically there at home, but I’m somewhere else in my head, replaying everything I think I did wrong. My wife will be talking and I’ll drift, or the kids will want my attention and I’ll give them this half-answer, and then five minutes later I’m mad at myself for doing it.\n\nAnd it’s not just home. Work kind of feeds into it too—if I let a forecast slide or I’m not fully prepared for a client thing, my team has to cover the gaps. Then I carry that shame home, and I’m even less present there. So… yeah, I think the guilt is probably hurting my relationships, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your guilty feelings are triggered by a specific, recurring thought that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think there is. It’s not like one exact sentence, but it’s this loop of, “You’re selfish, you keep letting them down.”\n\nAnd then my brain starts pulling up evidence, you know? Like every late night I stayed at work, or those tailgates I went to, or some dumb purchase I made and brushed off at the time. Even stuff that maybe wasn’t that big, it gets added to the pile.\n\nIt’s weird, because I’ll be doing something normal, like sitting at the kitchen table or driving past the stadium—sorry, that sounds random, but those tailgates are tied into it—and suddenly I’m back to thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who can’t get his priorities straight. Like they deserved better than me. That’s the part I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning as a result of your guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I guess. I still go to work, I still come home, I’m not just disappearing or anything.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. At work, if I get stuck in my head, my forecasts aren’t as tight as they should be, or I’m not as prepared for a client meeting as I normally would be. And then my team has to kind of cover the gaps, which just makes me feel worse.\n\nAnd at home… I’m there, but not the way I used to be. My wife can tell when I’m checked out, and the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I barely heard them. I hate that. I keep thinking about how my dad used to come home and just sit in his chair with the TV on—sorry, that’s probably not the point—but I catch myself doing that same kind of thing, just not really present. So yeah, it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about things outside of work and family as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it comes back to work and family, honestly. I mean, those are the big ones.\n\nBut it can spill over into other stuff too—like if I’m at a tailgate, or even just thinking about the money I spent on something stupid, I start doing this inventory in my head. Every late night at work, every tailgate, every dumb purchase… and somehow it all points back to the same thing, like, “See, you’re selfish.”\n\nAnd then I’m not just feeling bad about that one thing. I’m thinking my wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down. So even if it starts outside of work or family, it usually ends up there.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific mistakes or behaviors from your past that you keep coming back to when you feel guilty or ashamed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are a few that I keep circling back to.\n\nLate nights at work are a big one. Even if I told myself it was for the family, I’ll replay it like, “You chose work again.” And then there were tailgates I went to where, looking back, I feel like I should’ve been home or doing something useful. I know that probably sounds small, but in my head it becomes this whole case against me.\n\nAnd dumb purchases too—stuff I brushed off at the time, like, “It’s fine, I work hard,” but later I’ll think about what that money could’ve been for. Then it all kind of piles up, and I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of looping on the same examples, but those are the ones my brain keeps dragging back out.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular area of your life where you feel most guilty or ashamed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s mostly around being a husband and dad. Work is part of it too, but it all kind of funnels back to them.\n\nLike I’ll start thinking about every late night I stayed at work, or some tailgate I went to, or money I spent on something dumb, and then it turns into this whole… I don’t know, evidence pile. Like I’m building a case against myself that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then I end up thinking my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. Even saying that sounds dramatic, because nobody’s sitting me down and saying that to me, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about a specific thought or idea that triggers your feelings of guilt and shame?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there is kind of one thought, I guess. It’s not like a single sentence every time, but it’s that same idea that I’m selfish, that I’ve been selfish.\n\nLike I’ll replay every late night at work, every tailgate I went to, every dumb thing I bought when I probably should’ve been thinking about the family. And then my mind just… connects all of it, even stuff that doesn’t really belong together.\n\nI’ll be driving or trying to do something totally normal, and suddenly it’s like, “Your wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps letting them down.” That’s the one that really gets me. I know I keep saying it, but it just keeps coming back around."
